:

Figure 6:
Virtual corrections in the 3 approximations: CM (up),
\text{NWA}_{1}
(middle) and
\text{NWA}_{2}
(down).

The one-loop virtual amplitude at order
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3}\alpha_{s})
is derived by combining the weak Born-level Feynman diagrams (sub-figure
2a
) with their one-loop QCD corrections, which include diagrams containing at least one virtual gluon. These corrections consist of triangle, box, and pentagon diagrams with two or more strong vertices. Some examples are shown in the first line of figure
6
. All necessary UV counterterms are included to suppress the ultraviolet divergences in this amplitude.
MadGraph
can manage this automatically once the renormalized model in
UFO
format is implemented, which we have generated automatically using
FeynRules
.

The loop diagrams shown in the second (2-to-2 process) and third (2-to-1 process) rows of figure
6
are the virtual correction to the Born diagrams in sub-figures
2b
and
2c
, respectively. These diagrams are needed to obtain the NLO predictions in NWA
1
and NWA
2
approaches. We emphasize that the branching ratios remain unchanged in this case, specifically
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z}}=\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{H}}=0.5
and
\xi_{{Tb}}^{{W^{\prime}}}=0.2
and
0.05
for the DD and CSSM scenarios, respectively. Although it might seem necessary to compute the widths of
T
and
{W^{\prime}}
at NLO, this is actually not required, as the branching ratios of these particles are treated as free parameters within our model, serving as inputs rather than quantities derived from the calculated widths. We also note that the one-loop diagrams with a virtual gluon connecting the initial and final state partons (as it is the case of the pentagonal graph and some boxes in the 1st row of figure
6
) are color suppressed, because they are proportional to the trace of a Gell-Man matrix.

Figure 7:
Real emission diagrams: CM (up),
\text{NWA}_{1}
(middle) and
\text{NWA}_{2}
(down).

The interference of Born and virtual diagrams has infrared (soft/collinear) divergences. To preserve perturbative consistency, diagrams with the real emission of an additional parton (order
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3}\alpha_{s})
) must be included. The real emission processes that we have to consider are:

\displaystyle q\bar{q}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left[b\bar{t}H/Z+\bar{b}tH/Z\right]\,g,

\displaystyle qg

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left[b\bar{t}H/Z+\bar{b}tH/Z\right]\,q,

\displaystyle\bar{q}^{\prime}g

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left[b\bar{t}H/Z+\bar{b}tH/Z\right]\,\bar{q}^{\prime}.

(21)

In figure
7
, we depict some of the real emission Feynman diagrams associated with the Born process shown in figure
2
. The first process in eq. (
21
) arises from the Born diagrams with the emission of an extra gluon from the initial or final state quarks, cf. the first 3 diagrams of the first row of figure
7
. Since both initial state partons are massless, then initial state emission can be soft and collinear divergent. The same thing is true for the emission from the final sate, only if the emitting parton is massive (i.e.
T
or
t
), where only soft divergences are encountered. The soft divergences of the squared amplitude of these diagrams are canceled once we integrate over gluon momentum and combine it with virtual contribution, thanks to the Kinoshita-Lee-Nauenberg theorem (KLN)
[
75
,
76
]
. As for real emission processes initiated by gluon, they must be added because they contribute to quark structure functions (PDFs) scaling violation, they might contain initial state colinear singularities. After combining virtual and real emission amplitudes, only initial states colinear singularities remain, which are factorized in the NLO parton distribution functions, thanks to the factorization theorem
[
77
,
78
]
. Rows 2 and 3 of figure
7
show real emission diagrams corresponding, respectively, to
2\rightarrow 2
and
2\rightarrow 1
Born diagrams in figure
2
, i.e.
\text{NWA}_{1}
and
\text{NWA}_{2}
approximations, respectively.

As we have said above, one of the major motivations for considering NLO calculations is the reduction of the dependency on the non-physical scales of the cross section
16
16

16

Due the importance of the automation of the NLO and the next-to-next-leading order (NNLO) calculations
[
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
]
. To explicitly demonstrate this, we plot the variation of the inclusive cross sections, for
m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}=3/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=3.5\,\text{TeV}
, in the 3 approximations: CM (
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO/NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow btH/Z}
),
\text{NWA}_{1}
(
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO/NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow bT}
) and
\text{NWA}_{2}
(
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO/NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow W^{\prime}}\times\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tb}^{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
) in term of
\mu/\mu_{0}
, where
\mu_{F}=\mu_{R}=\mu
and the central scale
\mu_{0}
is the sum of transverse mass of final state particles divided by 2 (
\mu_{0}=H_{T}/2
), see figure.
8
.

Figure 8:
Scale variation of LO and NLO cross sections in the 3 approximations: CM,
\text{NWA}_{1}
and
\text{NWA}_{2}
.

We observe that, for the 3 approximations, the LO cross sections (
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow btH/Z}
,
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow bT}
and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow W^{\prime}}\times\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tb}^{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
) exhibit a stronger sensitivity to scale variation, behaving as monotonically decreasing functions on
\mu/\mu_{0}
. In contrast, the NLO cros sections (
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow btH/Z}
,
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow bT}
and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow W^{\prime}}\times\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tb}^{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
) show significantly reduced dependence, particularly at higher scales. It should be noted, however, that for very small scales, the LO and NLO predictions become very sensitive to scale variations. In this regime, the reliability of the predictions is compromised, as the calculations enter the non-perturbative QCD domain where the perturbative framework is no longer valid. Thus, the scale dependence is extremely reduced for NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM scheme, at NLO.

Figure 9:
Variation of LO and NLO cross sections in term of
m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
for DD scenario (up) and CSSM scenario (down).

In figure
9
, we illustrate the variation of the LO and NLO cross sections in CM,
\text{NWA}_{1}
and
\text{NWA}_{2}
approaches as functions of
m_{W^{\prime}}
for
m_{{}_{T}}=\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{2}{3}
and
\frac{3}{4}\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
in the DD and CSSM scenarios. The first observation that draws our attention is that all the LO and NLO cross sections behave similarly (all increase with
m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
17
17

17

They decrease when the masses increase because the phase space becomes more restricted.
) and are roughly comparable for the 3 employed approximations (CM, NWA
1
and NWA
2
) and the 3 adopted
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
hierarchies. To examine this more closely, we provide some numerical values for those cross sections, in DD scenario, associated with their scale and PDF uncertainties in table
4
. We mention that the numerical values of the cross section are displayed in the format
\sigma\,_{-\text{scal}\%}^{+\text{scal}\%}\,{}_{-\text{pdf}\%}^{+\text{pdf}\%}
, where
\pm\text{scal}\%
and
\pm\text{pdf}\%
represent the scale and PDF uncertainties in percentage, respectively.
From this table, we find that the scale uncertainties are extremely reduced in NLO compared to LO, confirming the conclusion drawn from figure
8
. Regarding the PDF uncertainties, they are almost the same for LO and NLO, however, they increase remarkably for higher
{W^{\prime}}
masses (from
5\%
for
m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}=2.5
TeV to
31\%

m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}=4.0
TeV). This is due to the lack of PDF constraints at high energy scales. The predictions of the NWA
2
are completely independent of
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
, which is expected, as the cross section in this case is given by
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO/NLO}}^{\scriptscriptstyle pp\rightarrow W^{\prime}}\times\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tb}^{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
and
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tb}^{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
is treated as a free parameter in our model. The LO predictions from CM scheme, NWA
1
and NWA
2
are nearly identical, with relative difference not exceeding
2\%
in all the cases. However, the NLO predictions for NWA
1
and NWA
2
are not as close to CM scheme predictions in all configurations. The largest relative differences are rerecorded for the high-mass case (
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=3/4\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
), about
8\%
for NWA
1
and
3\%
for NWA
2
, while the smallest differences are recorded for the low-mass case (
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=1/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
), about
4.75\%
for NWA
1
and
2.75\%
for NWA
2
. This is due to the fact that in the former case, we are closer to the decay threshold than in the latter case, see appendix
C
for more details.

The discussion above suggests that the NWA
2
approximation remains valid at NLO order, as it does at LO, for the DD (and CSSM) scenario, with about an accuracy of about
3\%
compared to the full approach (i.e. the CM scheme) for all the considered VLQ mass hierarchies
18
18

18

We show, in appendix
C
that NWA
2
and CM discrepancies may exceed
10\%
for scenarios other than those considered here.
.

A natural question arises: which of the NLO approximations is the most suitable to adopt? The ideal choice is the one that is least dependent on nonphysical scales, offers the highest precision and requires the lowest computational cost. Regarding scale dependence, the 3 NLO approximations (CM, NWA
1
and NWA
2
) show similar behavior, as their results converge at higher scales, as illustrated in figure
8
and exposed in table
4
. Concerning the precision, the NWA
1
is the less due to the important discrepancy with the full calculation predictions.
With respect to the fastest and the least time-consuming option, the NWA
2
appears to be the best, as it shows a significant reduction in computation cost, especially compared to CM option
19
19

19

At NLO, the
\text{NWA}_{2}
option takes few minutes despite the CM approach requires several hours for certain kinematic configurations, if a precision of 0.001 is requested in
MadGraph
.
. This is because it involves fewer loop diagrams and simpler topologies compared to the CM approach (full calculation), which is the least favorable in this context due to the increasing number of loop diagrams and more complex topologies which lead to increased computational demands.

Therefore, adopting
\text{NWA}_{2}
is advantageous, as it not only simplifies the calculations but also reduces the number of free parameters to just two:
\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{W^{\prime}}}
and the mass of
W^{\prime}
, which are sufficient to control the cross section in the DD scenario. We strongly emphasize that this conclusion is only valid for the DD scenario, as for further scenarios the discrepancy between NWA
1
and CM scheme may be large. In general, the CM scheme remains the most reliable and accurate method, despite its high computation cost, cf. appendix
C
.

Now, we are ready to compare our NLO predictions to CMS experimental data available at
https://www.hepdata.net/record/ins2039384
. In figure
10
, we present the upper observed limit at
95\%
confidence level (black curve) and the predictions of our model at NLO for various values of
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{W^{\prime}}}$}=0.2,0.3,\cdots,0.8
(dotted, dashed, dotted-dashed green, blue and red curves), in the DD scenario (the
W^{\prime}
boson decays equally to all type of quarks). Here, the lower limit on the
W^{\prime}
exhibits a strong dependence on only one free parameter which is
\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{W^{\prime}}}
. Specifically, the lower mass limit is approximately
2.8
TeV for
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{W^{\prime}}}$}=0.3
and increases to
3.8
TeV for
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{W^{\prime}}}$}=0.8
. Regarding the case where
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{W^{\prime}}}$}=0.2
, the
W^{\prime}
mass is not constrained by this data.

Figure 10:
NLO predictions versus data:
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=1/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
(left),
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=2/3\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
(middle) and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=3/4\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
(right).

Another legitimate question which might be asked: do the 3 approximations: CM, NWA
1
and NWA
2
, give the same differential distribution when parton shower is taken into account? To answer this question, we produced the differential cross section on the invariant mass of the final state leading jets, for
m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}=3.5
TeV,
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=1/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
and
\sqrt{s}=13\,
TeV in DD and
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,W^{\prime}\}}}
scenarios, see figure
11
.

Figure 11:
LO and NLO invariant mass differential distribution, for the low-mass case, in DD scenario

The invariant mass distribution is produced by using
MadAnalysis
[
85
]
and
FastJet
[
86
]

for jet finding. To reduce the unwanted events, we impose the following cuts on the final-state jets:
p_{\scriptscriptstyle{Tj}}>20
,
|\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{j}}|<5
and
\Delta R_{\scriptscriptstyle{jj}}>0.4
(
j
denotes both light-jets and b-jets). We observe that the differential distributions in NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM (both at LO and NLO) are globally similar, the only difference resides on the height of the peaks. At LO, the peaks are quite similar. However, at NLO, the CM scheme have relatively lower peak compared to those of NLO NWA
1
and NWA
2
. This is because the CM scheme take into account the off-shell effects (implemented through the Breit-Wigner propagators of
{W^{\prime}}
and
T
) and interference of the resonant (and non-resonant) contributions, which are completely absent in NWA
2
and partially included in NWA
1
. One might expect that the LO NWA
2
distribution should show a delta-function peak at
M_{\text{jets}}=m_{\scriptscriptstyle W^{\prime}}
. Nevertheless, this is not the case, as the distribution is smeared by parton-shower, specifically by initial-state radiation (ISR) and final state radiation (FSR).

3.3
Search for
{Z^{\prime}}
boson decaying into vector-like quark

The first search for heavy vector neutral boson decaying into VLQ at the LHC was performed by CMS collaboration in ref.
[
52
]
, where exclusion upper limits ranging between 0.13-10 pb are set on the cross section at 95% confidence level. In this sub.sec, we investigate the production and decay to VLQ of the
{Z^{\prime}}
boson in the context of our model, considering the benchmark scenarios
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
and
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
at LO and NLO using the 3 approximations employed in the previous subsection (NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM scheme), and considering the mass ratios:
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=1/2
(low-mass),
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=2/3
(middle-mass) and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=5/6
(high-mass) as adopted in ref.
[
52
]
.

The technical details are largely similar to those presented in sub.sec.
3.2
. Therefore, we will not go into all the details, but we will focus on presenting and analyzing the obtained results in the benchmark scenarios
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
and
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
. Here also, the dominant production mechanism of the
{Z^{\prime}}
boson at the LHC (which decays to
T
and subsequently to
tZ/H
) is the Drell-Yan process:
q\bar{q}\rightarrow\mbox{${Z^{\prime}}$}\rightarrow\bar{t}\,T+t\,\bar{T}\rightarrow t\,\bar{t}\,Z(H)
. The Born-level, the one-loop and real emission Feynman diagrams associated to this process are similar to those depicted, respectively, in figures
2
,
6
and
7
, where, without loss of generality, one simply replaces
{W^{\prime}}
with
{Z^{\prime}}
.

The ratio of the total width-to-mass of the
{Z^{\prime}}
depends only on the 2 parameters
\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
and
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{Tt}}
, it is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma^{\text{ToT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}}{m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma[Z^{\prime}\rightarrow T\bar{t}+\bar{T}t]/\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{Tt}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}}{m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}}=\frac{g^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\frac{1-2\,s_{\scriptscriptstyle{w}}^{2}+8/3\,s_{\scriptscriptstyle{w}}^{4}}{c_{\scriptscriptstyle{w}}^{2}}\,\frac{\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}$}^{2}}{1-\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{Tt}}}

(22)

Here also, we note that for
\xi_{{Tt}}^{{Z^{\prime}}}\leq 0.5
and
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}$}\leq 1
,
\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ToT}}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
is always less than
5.1\%
and the values of
\kappa_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
are always smaller than those given in table
2
, which preserves perturbative unitarity regardless the values of the masses
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
, see table
5
.

Numerical values of

for
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}$}=0.5,1,2
and

.

We notice that the coupling of
{Z^{\prime}}
to
T
and
t
is controlled by the coefficient:
\mbox{$\kappa^{\scriptscriptstyle{(Z^{\prime})}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{L}}$}=\kappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}\sqrt{\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{tT}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}/\Gamma_{\scriptscriptstyle{tT}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}}
, cf. eq. (
5
). It should be emphasized that the parameters
\kappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
and
\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
are not independent in the SSM, as it has been shown in appendix
A
. So, the free parameters that control the cross sections are:
\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
, the branching ratio
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{tT}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
, the mass of the
{Z^{\prime}}
boson (
m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
) and the mass of the vector-like quark (
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
).
We note also that we assumed, in this section, that
Z^{\prime}
decays democratically to up quarks (i.e.
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{tT}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{uu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{cc}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{tt}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
). Thus, this implies that in the SSM we must have
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle{tT}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=9.6\%
, see appendix
A
for more details.

We remind that the main motivation for considering NLO corrections in the approaches NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM scheme is to reduce the cross section’s dependence on unphysical scales compared to LO calculations. This is confirmed in figure
12
, where we show the variations of the LO and NLO cross sections with respect to
\mu/\mu_{0}
. Here, the factorization and renormalization scales are fixed such that
\mu_{F}=\mu_{R}=\mu
with
\mu_{0}=H_{T}/2
, for
m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}=2.5
TeV in the middle-mass case
m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}=3/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}
.
We observe that the predictions are quite sensitive to the scale
\mu
at LO compared to NLO as expected (in NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM). This is due to the fact that, in the latter one the virtual corrections and the real emission contribution are included which therefore makes the cross section more stable and significantly reduce the theoretical uncertainties associated to the renormalization and factorization scales. Concerning the scale dependency at NLO, the 3 approaches show approximately the same scale dependence, where the results converge for
\mu/\mu_{0}\geq 1
. As in the previous case, the less and the most CPU-time consuming options are, respectively, the NWA
2
and the CM scheme.

Figure 12:
Scale variation of LO and NLO cross sections for NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM scheme in the scenario
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
.

Other important parameters controlling the cross section are the mass
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
and the ratio
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
.
In fig
13
, we present the variation of the cross section in term of
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
within the 3 previously discussed approximations,
for:
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}=1/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}},m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}=2/3\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}=5/6\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
. These results are obtained for
\mbox{$\varkappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}$}=1
(i.e. CSSM) and
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{Tb}}=9.6\%
for center of mass energy
\sqrt{s}=13
TeV, in the benchmark scenario
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
. We observe that all the cross sections (LO and NLO) decrease when the mass of
{Z^{\prime}}
increase and consequently the mass of
T
increase, as expected. This occurs because the phase space becomes more limited as the masses of these particles increase. The same conclusions are obtained for the benchmark scenario
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
for which we did not include the plots to lighten the reading of the paper.

m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
in the benchmark scenario
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
.

As matter of fact, these plots in fig .
13
do not show how mach are the differences between the preidction of NWA
1
, NWA
2
and CM scheme. To illustrate these discrepancies, we give some numerical values of the cross sections for the benchmark scenarios
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
and
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
in
tables
6
and
7
, respectively. We observe that the cross sections in NWA
2
are insensitive to the ratio
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
, which is expected since the VLQ intervene only through the branching ratios
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tt}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
and
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Z/H}
. We recall that these quantities are treated as free parameters in our model, i.e. they are fixed for all masses and approximations (LO, NLO , …etc.) as
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Tt}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}=0.096
(DD to up quarks) and
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Z}=1
(in
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
) or
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle Z}=\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle H}=1/2
(in
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
), this justifies why the NWA
2
predictions in the benchmark scenarios
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,H,Z^{\prime}\}}}
and
{\bf T^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\{Z,Z^{\prime}\}}}
are approximately the same. However, in the other approaches (NWA
1
and CM), the predictions depend on that ratio due to the phase space restrictions and the kinematics which effect directly the total width of the VLQ in each case.

In all the results presented above, the widths of the unstable particles
{Z^{\prime}}
and
T
are narrow, with width-to-mass ratios not exceeding
6\%
. Nevertheless, tables
6
and
7
clearly shows that the results differ by few percent across the 3 approximations. To quantify this discrepancy, we will analyze how the cross sections vary with the width-to-mass raios of
{Z^{\prime}}
and
T
, and check whether the NWA ramain valid in all cases or not.

In figure
14
, we show the variation of the cross section, for
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}=2.5\,
TeV, in terms of
\Gamma^{\text{ToT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{T}}
(up panels) and
\Gamma^{\text{ToT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}/m_{\scriptscriptstyle{Z^{\prime}}}
(down panels). In the left, central and right panels respectively, we provide the variation of the cross sections for the low-, middle- and high-mass cases (i.e.
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=1/2\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
,
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=2/3\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle T}=5/6\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle Z^{\prime}}
). The sub-panels of each figure represent the relative difference (in percentage) between NWA
1
and NWA
2
cross sections and CM scheme cross sections, which are defined by:

\displaystyle\text{NWA}_{1}:

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma/\sigma[\%]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 28.45274pt}|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{NWA}_{1}}-\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{CM}}|/\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{CM}}\times 100\equiv\Delta\tilde{\sigma}[\%]

\displaystyle\text{NWA}_{2}:

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma/\sigma[\%]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 28.45274pt}|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{NWA}_{2}}-\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{CM}}|/\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{CM}}\times 100\equiv\Delta\tilde{\sigma}[\%]

(23)

with

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{CM}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\scriptstyle pp\rightarrow ttZ/H}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{NWA}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\scriptstyle pp\rightarrow tT}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}\times\text{Br}[T\rightarrow tZ/H]

\displaystyle\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}^{\text{NWA}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\scriptstyle pp\rightarrow Z^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{LO}/\text{NLO}}\times\text{Br}[Z^{\prime}\rightarrow tT]\times\text{Br}[T\rightarrow tZ/H]

(24)
